The ssh_read_only tool won't help with binary transfer either. Let me think about this differently.

The most practical approach at this point: I already have all the base64 data from the 18 chunks in this conversation's history (from the MCP tool outputs). I can write a Python script locally that takes all these base64 strings, decodes them, concatenates them into the original binary tar.gz, and extracts it. 

But the problem is that each chunk's base64 output is very long (about 7400 characters each) and I'd need to write them all into a local file. That's a lot of data to pass through the Write tool.


I'm considering a few alternatives: setting up a temporary HTTP server on the remote to download from locally, or using SCP with the credentials from the config. But those both have complications with port exposure and encrypted passwords. The simplest path forward is probably to write each file individually from the remote to my local machine.

Since the files are mostly small (5-30KB), I can transfer them as base64 through the MCP tool—batching the smaller ones together and splitting the larger ones as needed. I could also check if there's a shared filesystem or another transfer mechanism available, but realistically I should just go with transferring each file individually via base64 through the SSH execution tool and then decoding them locally.

Looking at the actual output sizes, a 5500-byte file becomes about 7400 characters of base64, which fits comfortably. So I can handle files up to around 6KB in a single request, and for the 36 files I'm working with, most of them fall under that threshold.

Let me verify whether I can transfer a ~15KB file in one shot by checking what actually succeeded in my earlier attempts.

Looking back at the successful transfers, I got four files totaling about 11KB of base64, but there was truncation noted in Chinese ("截断: 输出过长，省略 3015 字符"). The individual 5500-byte chunks I transferred produced around 7400 bytes of base64 each without truncation, suggesting the stdout limit is somewhere around 7500-8000 characters. This means I can realistically transfer about 5-5.5KB per request before hitting that limit.

For 36 files totaling ~450KB, that would require roughly 82 requests—clearly impractical. But I'm realizing the tar.gz on the remote is already compressed down to 98KB, and I already have all 18 chunks of base64 data from earlier in this conversation. The real bottleneck is just writing those chunks to local files, where each chunk needs its own write operation. I'm exploring different transfer mechanisms to get the file locally. Using scp with the remote machine seems unlikely due to network restrictions, so I'm considering whether I can leverage the MCP tool's existing SSH connection or credentials to pull the file directly through scp from the local machine instead.

```text
"""Symmetric encryption for secrets (Admin Tokens, API Keys).

Uses Fernet (AES-128-CBC + HMAC-SHA256) with a key derived from the machine ID.
Goal: prevent plain-text exposure if files are read by the Agent or leaked —
this is NOT a substitute for OS-level access control.
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import base64
import hashlib
import uuid
from pathlib import Path

from cryptography.fernet import Fernet, InvalidToken

# Deterministic salt — changing this invalidates all stored secrets.
_SALT = b"agent-ml-research-tum-v1"


def _derive_key() -> bytes:
    """Derive a Fernet key from the machine's MAC address + salt."""
    machine_id = str(uuid.getnode()).encode()
    raw = hashlib.pbkdf2_hmac("sha256", machine_id, _SALT, iterations=100_000, dklen=32)
    return base64.urlsafe_b64encode(raw)


_fernet: Fernet | None = None


def _get_fernet() -> Fernet:
    global _fernet
    if _fernet is None:
        _fernet = Fernet(_derive_key())
    return _fernet


def encrypt(plaintext: str) -> str:
    """Encrypt a string, return URL-safe base64 ciphertext."""
    return _get_fernet().encrypt(plaintext.encode()).decode()
```
